People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t care about anything the way I used to. Like, I’ve got all my fishing stuff sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be itching to get out with it, checking lures and all that, but it’s just… sitting there.

And my woodshop too—I used to love making things out there, even just sanding a piece down, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.

Even with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.

Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.

Like my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.

Same with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t care about anything the way I used to. Like, I’ve got all my fishing stuff sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be itching to get out with it, checking lures and all that, but it’s just… sitting there.\n\nAnd my woodshop too—I used to love making things out there, even just sanding a piece down, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.

But then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.

So… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.

It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.\n\nBut “most days”… I don’t know, I don’t exactly keep track. It all kind of blends together after a while. Like at work, I’ll be on site looking at framing or talking with a subcontractor, and I can sound normal enough, you know? Then I get in the truck afterward and it’s just quiet, and I realize I’ve been carrying this weight around.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe. I guess it’s been there a lot. Most days, probably.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any stretch of 2 months or longer during this time when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.\n\nIt’s been going on for, what, about a year and seven months now… since my wife’s health stuff kind of turned everything upside down at home. I mean, there might’ve been a decent week here or there, or a day where I got distracted by work—like if a job site was running behind and everybody needed something from me—but mostly I don’t think I ever got back to feeling like my usual self for that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “worthless,” but that’s kind of where my head goes.

Like, if I miss one of my wife’s appointments or I forget something with the kids, I take it as this big sign that I’m failing them. And at work, if there’s a cost overrun or some safety issue comes up, even if there are a dozen reasons for it, I’ll sit there thinking, “Well, there it is. I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.

It’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. Like everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one in charge of anything.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t care about anything the way I used to. Like, I’ve got all my fishing stuff sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be itching to get out with it, checking lures and all that, but it’s just… sitting there.\n\nAnd my woodshop too—I used to love making things out there, even just sanding a piece down, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.

And then I get scared because it feels too close.\n\nSorry, I know you asked “right now.” Right now I’m not doing anything, but the thought has been close.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period like this that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone, you know? What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t care about anything the way I used to. Like, I’ve got all my fishing stuff sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be itching to get out with it, checking lures and all that, but it’s just… sitting there.\n\nAnd my woodshop too—I used to love making things out there, even just sanding a piece down, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.\n\nBut “most days”… I don’t know, I don’t exactly keep track. It all kind of blends together after a while. Like at work, I’ll be on site looking at framing or talking with a subcontractor, and I can sound normal enough, you know? Then I get in the truck afterward and it’s just quiet, and I realize I’ve been carrying this weight around.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe. I guess it’s been there a lot. Most days, probably.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any stretch of 2 months or longer during this time when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.\n\nIt’s been going on for, what, about a year and seven months now… since my wife’s health stuff kind of turned everything upside down at home. I mean, there might’ve been a decent week here or there, or a day where I got distracted by work—like if a job site was running behind and everybody needed something from me—but mostly I don’t think I ever got back to feeling like my usual self for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “worthless,” but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nLike, if I miss one of my wife’s appointments or I forget something with the kids, I take it as this big sign that I’m failing them. And at work, if there’s a cost overrun or some safety issue comes up, even if there are a dozen reasons for it, I’ll sit there thinking, “Well, there it is. I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. Like everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one in charge of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big plan, but on those early drives, before anyone’s awake and the roads are quiet, my mind goes there. I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t make it to wherever I’m supposed to be going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking about the steering wheel, like… what if I just turned off the road. That scares me, honestly, because it feels too close.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now, sitting here with you? I… I don’t think I’m about to do anything this minute.\n\nBut it’s there, if that makes sense. Like I said, it mostly hits me on those early drives, before anyone else is awake. I’ll catch myself thinking it’d be easier if I just didn’t make it there, and then my mind goes to the steering wheel, like steering off the road. And then I get scared because it feels too close.\n\nSorry, I know you asked “right now.” Right now I’m not doing anything, but the thought has been close.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period like this that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.\n\nBut “most days”… I don’t know, I don’t exactly keep track. It all kind of blends together after a while. Like at work, I’ll be on site looking at framing or talking with a subcontractor, and I can sound normal enough, you know? I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big plan, but on those early drives, before anyone’s awake and the roads are quiet, my mind goes there. I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t make it to wherever I’m supposed to be going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking about the steering wheel, like… what if I just turned off the road. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know how to measure that, honestly.

I mean, what’s “expected,” you know? When your wife’s health changes, the whole house changes. You stop having normal routines, and every plan depends on how she’s doing that day. Even little things, like grocery shopping or whether I should answer a work call in the garage so I’m not bothering her… it all gets tangled up.

So part of me thinks, yeah, of course I’d feel bad. Anybody would. But then another part of me wonders if I’m making too much of it, or if I’m handling it worse than I should. I can’t really tell where the normal reaction ends and… whatever this is begins.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I just don’t care about anything the way I used to. Like, I’ve got all my fishing stuff sitting out in the garage, and normally I’d be itching to get out with it, checking lures and all that, but it’s just… sitting there.\n\nAnd my woodshop too—I used to love making things out there, even just sanding a piece down, you know? Now it feels like another job. Which is funny, I guess, because I manage construction crews all day, and you’d think I’d be used to things feeling like work, but this is different.\n\nEven with my kids, when they ask me to play chess, I’ll sit there, but I’m not really in it. I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.\n\nBut “most days”… I don’t know, I don’t exactly keep track. It all kind of blends together after a while. Like at work, I’ll be on site looking at framing or talking with a subcontractor, and I can sound normal enough, you know? Then I get in the truck afterward and it’s just quiet, and I realize I’ve been carrying this weight around.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe. I guess it’s been there a lot. Most days, probably.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any stretch of 2 months or longer during this time when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.\n\nIt’s been going on for, what, about a year and seven months now… since my wife’s health stuff kind of turned everything upside down at home. I mean, there might’ve been a decent week here or there, or a day where I got distracted by work—like if a job site was running behind and everybody needed something from me—but mostly I don’t think I ever got back to feeling like my usual self for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “worthless,” but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nLike, if I miss one of my wife’s appointments or I forget something with the kids, I take it as this big sign that I’m failing them. And at work, if there’s a cost overrun or some safety issue comes up, even if there are a dozen reasons for it, I’ll sit there thinking, “Well, there it is. I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. Like everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one in charge of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big plan, but on those early drives, before anyone’s awake and the roads are quiet, my mind goes there. I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t make it to wherever I’m supposed to be going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking about the steering wheel, like… what if I just turned off the road. That scares me, honestly, because it feels too close.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now, sitting here with you? I… I don’t think I’m about to do anything this minute.\n\nBut it’s there, if that makes sense. Like I said, it mostly hits me on those early drives, before anyone else is awake. I’ll catch myself thinking it’d be easier if I just didn’t make it there, and then my mind goes to the steering wheel, like steering off the road. And then I get scared because it feels too close.\n\nSorry, I know you asked “right now.” Right now I’m not doing anything, but the thought has been close.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period like this that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone, you know? I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. Like everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one in charge of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big plan, but on those early drives, before anyone’s awake and the roads are quiet, my mind goes there. I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t make it to wherever I’m supposed to be going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking about the steering wheel, like… what if I just turned off the road. That scares me, honestly, because it feels too close.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now, sitting here with you? I… I don’t think I’m about to do anything this minute.\n\nBut it’s there, if that makes sense. Like I said, it mostly hits me on those early drives, before anyone else is awake. I’ll catch myself thinking it’d be easier if I just didn’t make it there, and then my mind goes to the steering wheel, like steering off the road. And then I get scared because it feels too close.\n\nSorry, I know you asked “right now.” Right now I’m not doing anything, but the thought has been close.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period like this that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone, you know? Work gets bad, money stuff, family things piling up. In construction there’s always some season where everything goes sideways and you’re just exhausted and snapping at people, and you tell yourself it’ll calm down after the next inspection or after the next job closes out.\n\nBut a whole period like this, where it lasted weeks and felt… this heavy? Before this started? I’m not sure. I can’t really point to one and say, yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve had times where I had to push through on less sleep because of a job site or a deadline, you know, concrete’s getting poured at some stupid hour and everyone acts like the whole world ends if you’re not there.\n\nBut feeling like I didn’t need sleep, like I was wired in a good way or something… I’m not sure I’d say that. I don’t think so. I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by “unusually.”\n\nDoctor: Has there been a full 2-year period when you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: A full two years? No… I don’t think it’s been that long. It feels like forever, honestly, because once home changed it’s like everything else got rearranged around it, but if I’m counting right it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems changed everything at home. Before that I had bad days, sure, but this stretch… it’s been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like how bad this has gotten is more than you’d expect just from what’s been happening with your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to measure that, honestly.\n\nI mean, what’s “expected,” you know? When your wife’s health changes, the whole house changes. You stop having normal routines, and every plan depends on how she’s doing that day. Even little things, like grocery shopping or whether I should answer a work call in the garage so I’m not bothering her… it all gets tangled up.\n\nSo part of me thinks, yeah, of course I’d feel bad. Anybody would. But then another part of me wonders if I’m making too much of it, or if I’m handling it worse than I should. I can’t really tell where the normal reaction ends and… whatever this is begins.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… sleep’s been rough.

I’m just moving pieces around so they don’t feel bad. That’s kind of what brought me in, I guess. Nothing feels like something I’m looking forward to anymore.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it really started after my wife’s health stuff kind of changed everything at home. It’s hard to pin it to one day, you know? Because at first you’re just doing what needs doing—appointments, schedules, trying to keep the house moving—and then somewhere in there I guess I stopped feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s been… about a year and seven months now, give or take. Funny, I can remember because it was around when we were supposed to redo part of the back deck, and I had all the lumber priced out, and then that just never happened. So yeah, it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: If your wife’s health situation were to improve, do you think you’d still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’d like to think I’d snap back if she got better, but honestly, I’m not sure. It feels like I just stopped caring about the things I used to look forward to.\n\nLike my fishing gear is still just sitting there in the garage, untouched, and I walk past it all the time. I even bought this one lure a while back—supposed to be good for bass, guy at the shop talked my ear off about it—and normally I’d be itching to try it out. Now I just don’t.\n\nSame with the woodshop. It used to be where I’d go to clear my head, but now even that feels like a chore. And with the kids and chess, I’ll play if they ask, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. So… yeah, even if things improved, I don’t know that I’d feel like myself right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” is a big word, I guess. I mean, some days it feels like I’m just standing in the same spot, if that makes sense.\n\nBut then I start thinking, like, the deck out back still needs fixing, and I know exactly how I’d do it—what boards I’d replace first, what crew I’d put on a job like that if it weren’t my own house. Funny how I can plan that stuff for other people all day, but at home it just sits there.\n\nSo… I’m not sure if I’d call it hopeless. I guess I haven’t really thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… down, yeah, I guess that’s one word for it. Some mornings I’m just sort of there, moving through things.\n\nBut “most days”… I don’t know, I don’t exactly keep track. It all kind of blends together after a while. Like at work, I’ll be on site looking at framing or talking with a subcontractor, and I can sound normal enough, you know? Then I get in the truck afterward and it’s just quiet, and I realize I’ve been carrying this weight around.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe. I guess it’s been there a lot. Most days, probably.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any stretch of 2 months or longer during this time when you felt mostly back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not two months, anyway.\n\nIt’s been going on for, what, about a year and seven months now… since my wife’s health stuff kind of turned everything upside down at home. I mean, there might’ve been a decent week here or there, or a day where I got distracted by work—like if a job site was running behind and everybody needed something from me—but mostly I don’t think I ever got back to feeling like my usual self for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying “worthless,” but that’s kind of where my head goes.\n\nLike, if I miss one of my wife’s appointments or I forget something with the kids, I take it as this big sign that I’m failing them. And at work, if there’s a cost overrun or some safety issue comes up, even if there are a dozen reasons for it, I’ll sit there thinking, “Well, there it is. I’m the problem.” I’m supposed to be the one keeping things steady.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but on a job site you can usually trace things back—bad measurements, wrong material, someone didn’t read the plans right. And lately I feel like when something goes wrong, I trace it back to me. Like everyone would probably be better off if I wasn’t the one in charge of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like admitting that.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some big plan, but on those early drives, before anyone’s awake and the roads are quiet, my mind goes there. I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t make it to wherever I’m supposed to be going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I’ll catch myself thinking about the steering wheel, like… what if I just turned off the road. That scares me, honestly, because it feels too close.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now, sitting here with you? I… I don’t think I’m about to do anything this minute.\n\nBut it’s there, if that makes sense. Like I said, it mostly hits me on those early drives, before anyone else is awake. I’ll catch myself thinking it’d be easier if I just didn’t make it there, and then my mind goes to the steering wheel, like steering off the road. And then I get scared because it feels too close.\n\nSorry, I know you asked “right now.” Right now I’m not doing anything, but the thought has been close.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about a year and seven months ago, had you ever gone through another period like this that lasted at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, not like this, I don’t think.\n\nI’ve had rough stretches before, like anyone, you know? Work gets bad, money stuff, family things piling up. In construction there’s always some season where everything goes sideways and you’re just exhausted and snapping at people, and you tell yourself it’ll calm down after the next inspection or after the next job closes out.\n\nBut a whole period like this, where it lasted weeks and felt… this heavy? Before this started? I’m not sure. I can’t really point to one and say, yes, that was the same thing for two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt unusually energized or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve had times where I had to push through on less sleep because of a job site or a deadline, you know, concrete’s getting poured at some stupid hour and everyone acts like the whole world ends if you’re not there.\n\nBut feeling like I didn’t need sleep, like I was wired in a good way or something… I’m not sure I’d say that. I don’t think so. I guess I’d need to think about what you mean by “unusually.”\n\nDoctor: Has there been a full 2-year period when you felt down or depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: A full two years? No… I don’t think it’s been that long. It feels like forever, honestly, because once home changed it’s like everything else got rearranged around it, but if I’m counting right it’s been about a year and seven months.\n\nIt started around when my wife’s health problems changed everything at home. Before that I had bad days, sure, but this stretch… it’s been since then.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like how bad this has gotten is more than you’d expect just from what’s been happening with your wife’s health?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to measure that, honestly.\n\nI mean, what’s “expected,” you know? When your wife’s health changes, the whole house changes. You stop having normal routines, and every plan depends on how she’s doing that day. Even little things, like grocery shopping or whether I should answer a work call in the garage so I’m not bothering her… it all gets tangled up.\n\nSo part of me thinks, yeah, of course I’d feel bad. Anybody would. But then another part of me wonders if I’m making too much of it, or if I’m handling it worse than I should. I can’t really tell where the normal reaction ends and… whatever this is begins.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep’s been rough.\n\nI can be completely worn out when I get into bed, like physically done from the day, but then my head starts going. Bills, schedules, who’s supposed to be where, what I forgot to answer… stupid stuff too, like whether I moved a delivery or if I’m going to get a call first thing in the morning because somebody didn’t read an email.\n\nAnd if I do fall asleep, a lot of nights I’m up around three or four. Then I just kind of stare at the ceiling until it’s time to get up and drive to the site. Not really resting, just waiting.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
